- [x] #95301046 {difficulty:4} [runtime/compiler — a persistent worker pool, and parallelLoop on real threads (docs/parallelism-first-plan.md items 3-5)] [runtime/compiler — a persistent worker pool, and parallelLoop on real threads (docs/physics-performance-plan.md §5 item 2)] Today `parallelLoop` parses and runs as a plain loop, and every `spawn` is its own pthread. Box3D at 4 workers is 3.1-3.3x faster than at 1 and the featured physics example cannot meet its 4 ms budget without that (plan §2-§3). (1) A runtime worker pool: workers = performance cores (`hw.perflevel0.physicalcpu` on macOS — efficiency cores slow every spin barrier), created lazily, joined at exit, the calling thread participates; a parallel-for over an index range with fixed-size chunks claimed dynamically. (2) `parallelLoop` lowers to it: the body is outlined into a thunk; it may write the element of its own index (docs/concurrency-model.md §2b) and, new, the per-worker scratch slot of its `workerIndex` (Box3D merges per-worker bitsets, pair keys and events in worker order); anything else is a compile-time diagnostic. Results are deterministic and identical to the sequential loop for any worker count (fixtures: per-chunk partial sums combined in chunk order, a per-element transform over 1M elements, per-worker scratch merged in worker order, the diagnostics). (3) A guaranteed-concurrent mode — exactly one iteration per worker, all live at once — which the solver's spin barriers need (plan §5 item 3; a waiting worker must never depend on an iteration that has not started). (4) Measure the launch cost (target: microseconds — the physics step has ~10 launches outside the solver) and the speedup on a pure-compute kernel; record both in docs/concurrency-model.md. RAE_WORKERS=1 forces sequential. How the body names workerIndex and scratch is new surface: write it up first and ask ([question]) if it is more than a library function. Needs the TSan task. — LANDED (commit below): (1) runtime worker pool in runtime_threads.c — workers = performance cores (hw.perflevel0.physicalcpu, 8 here), caller participates, lazy start, joined at exit, RAE_WORKERS=n (1 = sequential), chunks claimed through one atomic word packing generation + chunk index, workers spin ~tens of us after a job then sleep (idle: 0.0% CPU), nested or concurrent launches run inline; (2) `parallelLoop var i: Int = start, i < end, ++i { }` lowers to rae_parallel_for with the body outlined into a C function and the used locals passed by address (c_stmt.c emit_parallel_loop); sema rejects any other loop shape and every write two iterations could share (shared assign/field/++, mod or own of a shared value incl. List.add, a mod alias, any index but the loop's own, ret / break / continue of the loop) — fixture 945 shows all 10 diagnostics; fixture 944 (1M-element transform, per-chunk partial sums combined in chunk order, structs via modAt, Strings per element, nested loop) prints identical bytes at 1/2/4/8 workers, leaks nothing, and is on the TSan gate (clean). Also fixed: count_ident_refs ignored `if let` bindings (a param read only there was miscounted). (4) benchmarks/parallel_loop: launch 0.27/0.62/3.9 us back to back and 4.7/7.8/15.9 us after 2 ms idle at 2/4/8 workers; compute kernel 1.98x / 3.82x / 5.96x, identical checksums (load ~6). lib/Parallel.rae workerCount(). Docs: concurrency-model.md §5-§6, physics-performance-plan.md item 2. Full suite 597/597 (incl. TSan 9 programs). Version 0.1.103. ANSWERED 2026-10-03 and closed as landed minus (3): (a) no workerIndex rule — per-worker scratch is chunk scratch (loop index = chunk with a contiguous slice and its own slot, merged in chunk order; byte-identical to sequential for any merge), fixture 946 (an order-sensitive concatenation at the chunk counts of 1/2/4/8 workers and on the pool; passes at RAE_WORKERS=1/2/4/8 and under TSan), documented in concurrency-model.md §5; it also exposed and fixed a lowering bug — a captured `view Int` parameter was re-declared with the reference type instead of the parameter's plain type (emit_param_c_type now shared with emit_param_list). (b) the guaranteed-concurrent mode moved into the atomics/stages design task.
- [x] #67388129 {difficulty:4} [language design — atomics and staged parallel work for the physics solver: DESIGN, then [question]] [language design — atomics and staged parallel work for the physics solver: DESIGN, then [question]] docs/physics-performance-plan.md §4-§5 item 3. Box3D's solver keeps every worker inside ONE task across a few hundred stage barriers per step (11 stage kinds; warm start, solve and relax per colour, per substep): workers claim fixed-size blocks with a per-block atomic compare-and-swap on a monotonic sync index, count completions with an atomic counter, and spin with a CPU yield until the main thread publishes the next stage in one atomic word (~50 atomic call sites). Re-dispatching a pool task per stage would cost more than the stage, and Rae has no function values, so a library cannot take the block work as a callback. Recommended shape, to write up with a worked example from the contact solver: an `Atomic(Int)` / `Atomic(Int32)` library type over C11 stdatomic (load, store, fetchAdd, compareExchange; never on floats), a `cpuRelax()` intrinsic, and the worker pool's guaranteed-concurrent parallelLoop mode — the solver then runs Box3D's own stage loop, as the C does. Compare it honestly with the alternative, a dedicated stage/barrier construct that hides the atomics. ALSO design here the guaranteed-concurrent mode (every worker running one iteration at once — what spin barriers need; moved here 2026-10-03, since it only serves spin barriers and those need atomics): the design must state (1) its determinism story (identical bits for any worker count, like parallelLoop) and (2) its RAE_WORKERS=1 behaviour — one worker cannot have all iterations live at once, so barriers must degrade to sequential stages, never deadlock. Per-worker scratch is NOT part of this: it is chunk scratch (docs/concurrency-model.md §5, fixture 946), and no workerIndex rule is to be added. Until this lands the solver stays sequential and the parallelLoop checker rules stay as they are. List the compiler and runtime work, and end with [question] for a choice; the implementation is a follow-up task. Never @-attributes. The worker pool and parallelLoop have landed. — DESIGNED in docs/parallel-stages-design.md (nothing implemented). Measured first (benchmarks/parallel_stages: a 220-stage solver-shaped step, 32 blocks of ~1.5 us per stage, as one parallelLoop per stage vs one flat parallelLoop): barrier 1.3 / 1.4 / 4.7 us per stage at 2 / 4 / 8 workers — staged 1.85x / 3.38x / 4.33x vs flat 1.96x / 3.79x / 7.6x (Box3D's own C reaches 3.4x at 8 workers on the pyramid). So re-dispatching per stage does NOT cost more than the stage at 4 workers; at 8 the cost is the pool's single shared claim word, a runtime-only fix. Three options written up with determinism, RAE_WORKERS=1 behaviour, deadlock risk and compiler/runtime work: C (recommended) a stage is a parallelLoop — no new construct, no atomics, determinism by construction, sequential stages at RAE_WORKERS=1, cannot deadlock; follow-up is a runtime launch-path rewrite (per-worker start offsets, per-chunk claims, inline one-chunk stages, per-worker done flags) accepted when 8-worker barriers are ≤ ~1.5 us. A: Atomic(Int)/Atomic(Int32) + cpuRelax() + a parallelTeam keyword (the guaranteed-concurrent mode) running Box3D's loop line by line — determinism by three unchecked conventions, and it can deadlock (a leader that waits for a helper hangs at RAE_WORKERS=1); progress rule = Box3D's (iteration 0 on the caller can finish every stage alone, helpers only steal). B: a parallelStages construct = C's semantics with workers resident, only worth it if C's runtime fix falls short. Found while writing the worked example: EVERY option needs one more thing — the solver writes body states at indices from constraint data (disjoint only by graph colouring, which the checker cannot prove, and restructuring to own-index writes would break bit-exactness with the oracle). DECIDED 2026-10-03: Option C (a stage is a parallelLoop) with `unsafe` for the colouring's disjoint writes — and IMPLEMENTED: (1) `unsafe { }` in a parallelLoop body lifts the write rule (ret/break/continue stay rejected); every List set/modAt there reports its element to rae_parallel_check_write, and RAE_PARALLEL_CHECK=1 / Parallel.checkWrites(enabled: true) stops the program on two iterations writing one element ('iterations 3 and 5 both wrote parallel[7]'); fixture 947 (greedy colouring of 200 contacts on 64 bodies, one parallelLoop per colour, bit-identical to the sequential solve at 1/2/3/8 workers, then a wrong colouring caught). (2) launch path: per-worker contiguous chunk ranges with cache-line-padded claim words and stealing, one completion add per worker per job, one-chunk jobs inline, 250 us clock-based spin before sleeping, lock-free pool-start check; back-to-back launch at 8 workers 3.9 -> 2.0 us. Barrier per stage measured side by side with a Box3D-style C reference of the same step (benchmarks/parallel_stages/c/box3d_style.c, interleaved, machine at load 5-11): 4 workers Rae 1.4-1.7 us vs Box3D-style 0.55-1.3 us, 8 workers Rae 2.2-3.0 us (one 9.4 us outlier) vs 1.65-3.4 us — usually within 1.0-1.4x of Box3D's own scheme; the absolute 1.5 us at 8 workers is out of reach on this loaded machine for both. Side finding: the same block arithmetic is 2x slower in Rae than in C (scalar codegen, not threading). TSan clean at 2 and 8 workers; full suite 599/599. Docs: parallel-stages-design.md §9, concurrency-model.md §5, physics-performance-plan.md item 3. Version 0.1.104.

- [x] #52781647 {difficulty:4} [language design — a 4-wide float type (Float4) for SIMD: DESIGN plus a measured prototype, then [question]] [language design — a 4-wide float type (Float4) for SIMD: DESIGN plus a measured prototype, then [question]] docs/physics-performance-plan.md §2 and §5 item 4: Box3D's NEON build is 1.35-1.5x faster than scalar on contact-heavy scenes (5 050-box pyramid: 8.1 vs 11.8 ms per step single-threaded; 2.75 vs 3.8 ms at 4 workers), 1.1-1.2x on piles, 1.0x on joints — it is the margin that keeps the featured example within 4 ms unless the port is within ~1.05x of C. Design a `Float4` value type (lane-wise arithmetic, load/store from a List(Float), compare/select, horizontal ops) lowering to SSE2 / NEON / wasm SIMD128, with the scalar struct as the fallback. Box3D keeps its SIMD path bit-identical to the scalar one (no approximate rsqrt; convex_manifold.c 'needs to match the SIMD version'), so the Float4 contact solver must stay bit-exact against the scalar oracle. Prototype it on the contact-solver kernel extracted into benchmarks/ (scalar Rae vs Float4 Rae vs C SIMD) and record the numbers. End with [question] for approval of the surface. Needs P4 (the scalar contact solver exists). — DONE (design + prototype): docs/float4-design.md proposes a builtin Float4 (lane fields x y z w, lane-wise + - * /, named functions with scalar-exact semantics: compare-and-select min/max/abs, unfused mulAdd, no approximate rsqrt), a separate Mask4 for comparisons, defined-order horizontal ops, opt-returning load/store from List(Float), lowered to NEON / SSE2 / wasm SIMD128 inline intrinsics with the scalar struct as fallback. Prototype benchmarks/float4_solver (run.sh): the real P4 wide contact solver over a SIMD FloatWide is bit-exact with Box3D's scalar AND NEON builds and with the scalar Rae solver; Float4 is 1.32-1.35x faster than scalar Rae (C NEON vs C scalar: 1.62-1.66x); with a higher inline threshold Rae scalar is 0.96x C scalar and Float4 is within 1.15x of C NEON (load ~8 during the runs; numbers in §5). A native vector type is no faster than a struct with SIMD primitives: the win needs the primitives inline, not a special representation. QUESTION: approve the Float4 surface of docs/float4-design.md §2 (the four questions in §7: separate Mask4 vs masks as Float4; lane-wise operators vs named functions only; opt-returning load vs an Array-indexed form; living in the prelude lib/core)? On approval the compiler work and the FloatWide switch are §8. — ANSWERED 2026-10-04: approved with changes (docs/float4-design.md §0): Mask4 stays separate (select, and/or/not, any/all only); named functions only, no lane-wise operators; the load returns opt Float4; an imported module lib/Float4.rae, not the prelude. Implementation queued as '[compiler — implement Float4 / Mask4 and switch FloatWide over]'.
- [x] #85467200 {difficulty:4} [runtime — one String from readFile leaks when the first loop iteration returns it] `func readText() ret String { let candidates: List(String) = { "/etc/hosts", "/nonexistent" } loop var i: Int = 0, i < candidates.length, ++i { if let text: String = readFile(path: candidates.copyAtDefault(index: i)) { ret text } } ret "" }` called from main as `let golden: String = readText()` leaves that String outstanding at exit (RAE_MEM_STATS=1: read_file alloc=1 free=0); with the existing file SECOND in the list nothing leaks. The emitted C looks right (pool take / register_owned / drop of golden at exit), so suspect the string pool's mark/flush/register_owned interplay. Every physics fixture (948-954) reads its golden this way and leaks it. Add a leak fixture with rae_ext_rae_mem_stats_outstanding. — DONE: root cause in the compiler, not the string pool: emit_optional_boxed_expr wrapped an extern `ret opt String` result (rae_sys_read_file) with has=1 + rae_string_copy, leaking the native buffer on every read and turning a missing file into a present empty String; it now moves the String and maps NULL data to none. Fixture 960_read_file_opt (missing → none, empty file present, 200 reads leak 0 strings; 101 leaked before). Full suite 612/0. VERSION 0.1.115.
- [x] #49544702 {difficulty:4} [tests — the run-case leak check is blind: it reads the rae driver's own all-zero mem-stats dump] compiler/tools/run_tests.sh takes the LAST '[mem:string:TOTAL]' / '[mem:buf]' line (tail -1) of a `run` case's output under RAE_MEM_STATS=1. But the runtime's mem-stats constructor (rae_install_mem_stats in runtime/runtime_core_memory.c) also runs in the `rae` driver, which links the runtime, so after the compiled program's exit dump the driver prints its own 'cumulative since process start' block with alloc=0 — and that is what the check reads. Every case passes the gate regardless of leaks: fixture 951 leaked 3475 buffers before the opt-ret fix of 2026-10-04 and still PASSED under run_tests.sh. Fix: keep the driver from reporting (or read the program's block), then run the full suite and triage whatever turns red. The readFile String leak of the physics fixtures 948-959 is the task above (do it first); anything else that turns red is a real leak: fix small ones here, queue large ones as new tasks with what the counters show. — DONE: the driver now silences its own exit report (rae_mem_stats_silence_exit_report, called first in main), so the leak check reads the program's dump; it also skips programs that exit non-zero on purpose (840/841 abort with a live World). The real leaks it then caught are fixed: an `opt String` call result interpolated into a string was never freed (9 fixtures: 370 375 381 424 445 451 452 461 554); `ret text` of a String local from an `opt String` function copied the local and orphaned it (531 665 671); `let s: String = ident` was taken as an alias, so a caller cleared is_owned on the returned copy (all of lib Filesystem's path helpers, 544); FileWatch.checkForChanges rewrote entries through a raw buffer set and leaked the old path (415, now modAt in place); 527 freed its own unsafe buffers. Full suite 612/0 with the check live. VERSION 0.1.116.
- [x] #75798611 {difficulty:4} [compiler — a statement temporary of a heap-nested struct is dropped with the _alias drop and leaks] A call result used directly as an argument or field access, e.g. `contactEdge(contact: world.contacts.copyAtDefault(index: id), edgeIndex: 0)` or `world.contacts.copyAtDefault(index: id).flags`, is materialised into a statement temporary (`__rae_stmt_tmpN`) that is released with `rae_drop_struct_<T>_alias`. For a struct whose list elements own heap (lib/physics/dynamics Contact: `manifolds: List(Manifold)`, each Manifold with a points List) the alias drop is empty, so the deep copy copyAtDefault returned leaks: 2 buffers per destroyed contact before the physics code was changed to read contacts through viewAt (2026-10-04, fixture 957). A `let` binding of the same call gets the owning drop. Make statement temporaries of calls that return an owned value (copyAtDefault, any plain `ret T`) use the owning drop, keeping the alias drop only where the callee really returns an alias; add a leak fixture with rae_ext_rae_mem_stats_buf_outstanding. — DONE: only the `view`-argument temporaries were affected (a member read like `.flags` already got the owning drop); both argument sites in c_call.c passed owns_heap only for an object literal. New c_stmt_temp_value_owns: an object literal or a call that returns a value it made is owned; a buffer-get intrinsic or a callee whose body returns an alias (the same scan a `let` uses) keeps the alias drop. Fixture 961_stmt_temp_owned_call (copyAtDefault and plain `ret T` as arguments leak 0 buffers, was 200 per 200 temporaries; an alias-returning callee leaves the list intact). Full suite 613/0. VERSION 0.1.117.

Workaround in lib/physics/geometry/Hull.rae and Hull2d.rae: bind the element to a typed local first. Materialise such arguments into a temporary in the C emitter (as is already done for whole-struct rvalue arguments: `((rae_Vec3[1]){ ... })`); add a fixture. — DONE: c_call.c now routes a `view` argument that is a member chain read off a call (not a buffer-get intrinsic) whose struct holds no heap through the existing rvalue compound-literal temporary; a heap-holding result already went through a statement temporary (a place) and is unchanged. Fixture 962_view_arg_field_of_call (copyAtDefault field, nested field, function-result field, both arguments, heap-holding element). The typed-local bindings in Hull.rae/Hull2d.rae are ordinary code (each element is used more than once), so they were left as they are. Full suite 614/0. VERSION 0.1.118.
- [x] #97861858 {difficulty:4} [compiler — a view for-each over List(UInt64) emits invalid C] `loop let key: view UInt64 in keys { … }` (keys: List(UInt64)) compiles to `uint64_t* key = { .ptr = &…data[i] };` and clang rejects the designated initializer for a pointer. A view loop over a List of structs works; over UInt64 it does not (probably every fixed-width integer element type: check Int32/UInt32/UInt8 too). Found in compiler/tests/cases/953_physics_broad_phase, worked around with an index loop. Fix the element-binding lowering for scalar element types; add a fixture. — DONE: root cause was a mismatch: the C type of a `view`/`mod` reference is a `{ .ptr }` wrapper only for Int/Float/Float64/Bool/Char/String, a plain `T*` for Int8..UInt64, but several emitter sites assumed the wrapper for every primitive. New shared c_primitive_ref_has_wrapper (c_backend.c) now decides at the for-each binding, the list if-let binding, the ref-bind `=>` site, is_primitive_ref, assignment through a mod ref, the call-argument wrap (and its hoisted-temporary form) and the opt-ref none check; a mod fixed-width ident reads as `(*name)`. So `mod` of a fixed-width integer (parameters, their call sites, if-let modAt), which was broken everywhere, works too. Fixture 963_view_loop_fixed_width (UInt64/Int32/UInt32/UInt8/Int16 view loops, mod loop, if-let view/mod, mod UInt64 and Int32 parameters, mod forwarded to another call); 953 uses the view loop again and stays bit-exact. Full suite 615/0. VERSION 0.1.119. Found on the way, queued: UInt32 interpolation prints nothing.
- [x] #60145614 {difficulty:4} [compiler — overload resolution misses the type of a generic method call argument] `add(left: multiply(matrix: rotation, vector: proxyB.points.copyAtDefault(index: indexB)), right: translation)` fails with "argument 'matrix' does not match parameter 'left' of multiply(left: view Matrix3, ...)" and then tries List.add: when an argument of an overloaded call is a generic method call (List.copyAtDefault), its type is not known when the overload is chosen. Binding it to a typed local first works (the workaround in lib/physics/collision/Distance.rae). Fix the resolution order; add a fixture. — DONE: the cause was not ordering: sema analyses the arguments first, but a generic method of the PRELUDE (core/List copyAtDefault) was never typed, because the receiver-method search only looked in the module's own decls and its imports (and spelled the receiver as the mangled `List_Vec3`). An untyped argument matches any parameter, so the first `multiply` whose other parameter fit won. sema now gives such a call its result type when the method returns a bare `T` (the inferred argument's TypeInfo; the call stays unbound for the backend, as before, so nothing else changes). Fixture 964_overload_generic_method_arg (local list, field list nested in another overloaded call, the Matrix3×Matrix3 overload chosen correctly). Full suite 616/0; all 77 examples pass the emit-c compile check. Other generic return shapes (`opt T`, `List(T)`) stay untyped in sema as before. VERSION 0.1.120.
- [x] #79509597 {difficulty:4} [compiler — a bare module const resolves to the first module that declares the name, ignoring opens] In lib/physics/geometry/Sphere.rae a bare `pi` (physics/math/Scalar is open, lib/Math only imported) compiles to rae_g_Math_pi: there is one symbol table, so sema leaves no decl_link and ident_c_name (c_backend.c) picks the first global with that name, preferring only the function's own module. Resolve a bare global through the module's own decls and its `open`s (ambiguity between two opens = an error naming both), and set decl_link. Today harmless only because both pi/twoPi round to the same f32; add a fixture with two modules declaring the same const name with different values. — DONE: two causes. Symbol registration kept only the FIRST global of a name (a second module's const was skipped, and its folding wrote onto the first one's symbol), and a bare read took that first symbol. Now every same-name global gets its own symbol (behind the first, so plain lookups are unchanged), a global is analysed through its own symbol, and a bare read (and a const initializer) resolves through sema_resolve_bare_global: own module, else the one module open here, else the explicitly `open`ed one; two opened = an error naming both (suggesting `Module.name` only for a top-level module). The IDENT gets decl_link, so the backend emits that module's symbol: Sphere now reads physics/math/Scalar's pi. Fixtures 965_bare_const_by_open (lib WorldBiome vs procgen/Texture `matGrass` 3 vs 0, both arrangements, a const initializer, a module's own const; the old compiler read the IMPORTED module's value in both) and 966_bare_const_ambiguous. Full suite 618/0, 77 examples emit-c clean. VERSION 0.1.121. Queued: no qualified spelling for a package module's const; relative-path opens do not count as opens.
- [x] #14060407 {difficulty:4} [compiler/runtime — Math.sqrt and friends are calls into the runtime object] docs/float4-design.md §6.4: Math.sqrt lowers to rae_ext_Math_sqrt, an extern defined in the separately compiled runtime, so every scalar square root in hot code (the physics solver's friction and rolling clamps, vector lengths) is a real call that cannot inline. Make the pure scalar math externs (sqrt, and whichever of floor/ceil/abs/min/max/sin/cos are runtime calls) inline intrinsics in the generated C (static inline in rae_runtime.h, or direct libm/builtin calls), keeping the bits identical (sqrtf is IEEE); check fixtures 948-959 stay bit-exact and measure benchmarks/float4_solver. — DONE: sqrt, floor, ceil, round, remainder and floatBits/floatFromBits are static inline in rae_runtime.h (out of runtime_buffers_math.c); the generated prototype after them is compatible C, so nothing in the emitter changed. abs/min/max were already Rae functions. The transcendental ones (sin cos tan asin acos atan atan2 pow exp log) stay out of line on purpose: libm does not round them correctly, so a C compiler folding sinf(constant) with its own implementation (GCC/MPFR) could change the bits. Verified: the release rae_scalar binary has no sqrt call (3 fsqrt); fixtures 948-959 bit-exact at default and (948, 958) release profile; benchmarks/float4_solver rae_scalar 603.6 -> 588.1 ms (load ~4, indicative), all five checksums still match; noted in docs/float4-design.md §6.4. Full suite 618/0. VERSION 0.1.122.
- [x] #66010315 {difficulty:4} [compiler — release builds leave small Rae functions out of line] docs/float4-design.md §6.3: in benchmarks/float4_solver the release profile (-O2) leaves the physics solver's small helpers (applyWideImpulse, multiplySym3Wide, gatherBodies, …) out of line, passing structs through memory; -mllvm -inline-threshold=3000 makes the scalar Rae contact solver 0.96x C scalar instead of 1.15x and the Float4 prototype 1.15x C NEON instead of 1.43x. Measure the whole suite and the examples with a raised threshold (or emit small functions as static inline with an always_inline heuristic), check binary size and compile time, and pick the release default; record the numbers in docs/float4-design.md. — DONE: release default is now `-mllvm -inline-threshold=400` (clang only; added when rae itself was built by clang). Measured thresholds 225/300/400/500/1000/3000 on the float4_solver benchmark: 400 is where the gain stops (rae_scalar 1.14x -> 1.00x of C scalar, Float4 prototype 1.40x -> 1.16x of C NEON; bit-exact throughout). Cost over all 77 examples' generated C: +20% compile time (102 -> 123 s), +22% code (9.7 -> 11.9 MB); 3000 would be +96%/+95%; the runtime object +9%/+9%. The always_inline-on-small-functions alternative made the solver slower (622 vs 590 ms) and was dropped. New RAE_EXTRA_CFLAGS appends flags to the release compile for such measurements; run.sh mirrors the new default. Numbers in docs/float4-design.md §6.3. Full suite 618/0 (737 s, no same-load baseline to compare). VERSION 0.1.123.
- [x] #96583214 {difficulty:4} [compiler — the Float4 / Mask4 types (lib/Float4.rae) with a permanent bit-exactness fixture] docs/float4-design.md §0 is the APPROVED surface (it overrides §2): an imported module lib/Float4.rae (NOT the prelude) with the builtin lowering behind it. Float4: four Float lanes x y z w, readable like fields, built with the typed literal; NAMED FUNCTIONS ONLY, no operators: Float4.splat(value:), add/sub/mul/div(a:b:), mulAdd(a:b:c:) (never fused), sqrt, min/max (compare-and-select: a <= b ? a : b / a >= b ? a : b), abs (a < 0 ? -a : a, keeps -0), clampSymmetric(value:limit:), the defined-order horizontal ops (sum = ((x+y)+z)+w, horizontalMin/Max, minLaneIndex for the hull SAT's index-in-mantissa search), comparisons less/greater/lessOrEqual/greaterOrEqual/equal returning Mask4, Float4.select(mask:ifTrue:ifFalse:), Float4.load(from: view List(Float), index:) ret opt Float4 and store(into:index:value:) (out of range ignored with a warning, like List.set). Mask4 is its own type and exposes ONLY select (via Float4.select), and/or/not and any/all. Lower every function to inline code in the generated C — NEON (arm64), SSE2 (x86-64), wasm SIMD128 — with a struct of four floats and the scalar definitions as the fallback; never a call into the runtime object (benchmarks/float4_solver/c/float4_prototype.h is the reference lowering). A PERMANENT fixture checks every function's bits against its scalar definition, including -0, +-inf, NaN and denormals, and runs on the scalar fallback too (a build flag that forces it). Leave Vec4 alone (a separate, later ticket only if a measurement asks for it). The FloatWide switch is the next task. — DONE: lib/Float4.rae (Float4 lanes x y z w; Mask4) with every function an unsafe extern("rae_f4_*") implemented inline in compiler/runtime/runtime_float4.h: NEON, SSE2, wasm SIMD128 and the scalar definitions; the generated C includes that header only when a program uses Float4 (it cost ~4% of every runtime compile otherwise). `rae run --float4-scalar` forces the scalar path. Fixtures 967_float4_bit_exact (SIMD) and 968_float4_bit_exact_scalar check every function bit for bit against its scalar definition over -0, +-inf, NaNs, denormals, extremes and arbitrary mask bits. Verified on NEON, scalar and wasm SIMD128 (emcc + node, identical output); SSE2 compiles for x86-64 but could not be run here (no Rosetta). A release build of a Float4 loop compiles to fmul.4s/fadd.4s with no calls. Deviations from §0, recorded in docs/float4-design.md §0.1: and/or/not/any are keywords so the mask functions are both/either/invert/anyLane/allLanes; Mask4 lanes are UInt32 fields (no private fields in Rae); mulAdd(a,b,c) = a*b + c; with two or more NaN inputs the result is a quiet NaN equal to one of them (the C compiler may commute a scalar x + y, which the fixture caught); added negate, embedIndex (the other half of minLaneIndex) and lowering(). Full suite 620/0. VERSION 0.1.124.

- [x] #93440860 {difficulty:4} [compiler — assigning a local `opt` struct with heap into a field leaves the field dangling] `var built: opt Payload = none; built = makePayload(); holder.payload = built` (Payload has a List field, holder is `mod Holder` with `payload: opt Payload`) then reading holder.payload gives garbage (e.g. `values 5541 0` instead of `1 3`): the field gets a shallow copy and the local's drop frees the shared buffers. Assigning the call result directly (`holder.payload = makePayload()`) works. Old (reproduces at 975a0180). Hit in fixture 971 (worked around there). Make an assignment from an owning opt local into a field deep-copy (or move, if the local is not used after) like a plain struct; fixture under the leak check. — DONE: the opt assignment branch (c_stmt.c) deep-copies an IDENT/MEMBER/INDEX `opt T` source whose payload owns heap (inline has + rae_deep_copy of the payload, String via rae_string_copy) instead of passing the struct through; fixture 973_opt_local_into_field (local->field, replace, clear the source after, field->field, field->local->field, opt String) is leak-clean. - [x] #30945130 {difficulty:4} [compiler — a call nested in a List literal element computes the wrong value] In `let points: List(Vec3) = { add(left: multiply(matrix: matrix, vector: first), right: offset), ... }` (written vertically, as the formatter lays it out; `open Vec3`, `physics/math/VectorMath`, `physics/math/MatrixMath`, `matrix = matrixIdentity()`, first = (1,2,3), offset = (0,0,-5)) the element comes out (1, 0, -5): the inner `multiply(matrix:, vector:)` yields (x, 0, 0); the same expression bound with `let v: Vec3 = add(...)` gives (1, 2, -2), and `{ add(left: first, right: offset) }` without the nested multiply is right. Found porting mesh_contact.c (MeshContact.rae now builds its triangle from three lets). Fix the literal-element lowering (likely the element's expected type leaking into the nested call's overload/temporary), add a fixture under the leak check. — DONE: the cause was in sema, not the lowering: sema_analyze_expr had no case for AST_EXPR_COLLECTION_LITERAL (nor AST_EXPR_LIST), so a call in a literal element was never resolved and the backend picked the first same-named function (VectorMath.multiply(left:, right:) for MatrixMath.multiply(matrix:, vector:)). Both now analyse their elements; fixture 975_literal_element_overload (overloads differing only in argument names, nested, String elements) is leak-clean and fails on the old compiler. Two older bugs found on the way are queued below.
- [x] #38205845 {difficulty:4} [compiler — a nested List literal crashes the compiler] `let nested: List(List(Point)) = {` / ` { combine(scale: -1, point: first) }` / `}` (Point a plain {x: Int, y: Int} struct) makes `rae run` die with SIGSEGV/SIGBUS in emit_type_recursive (c_backend_emit_module), before and after the literal-element sema fix (#30945130). Find why the inner literal's type reaches emit_type_recursive malformed (c_discovery's nested-literal registration is the likely place), fix, add a fixture with List(List(T)) literals of structs and Strings under the leak check. — DONE: four faults, all fixed. (1) The crash: sema's `if let x: view T => ...` narrowing resolved a STACK AstTypeRef copy, and resolving a generic type registers that pointer for emission, so emit_type_recursive read a dead frame (any `if let inner: view List(T) => lists.viewAt(...)`, literal or not); now arena-allocated. (2) c_discovery registered `&ctx->expected_type` (a reused context field) the same way; now a stable copy. (3) A literal registered its list drop only when the elements own heap, but the outer list's drop calls it per element (`List(List(Point))` hit an undeclared `List_drop<Point>`); now always, and emit_specialized_function forward-declares the element container's drop before a container drop (List.create(List(Point)) had the same ordering error). (4) The nested-literal lowering (c_expr) did not detach String elements from the pool or move local elements, as the let lowering does (`{ "a {x}" }` inside a literal read freed memory). Fixture 976_nested_list_literals (structs, Strings, an interpolation, a moved String local, three levels, viewAt narrowing, List.create + modAt) is leak-clean.
- [x] #13383718 {difficulty:4} [compiler — chained copyAtDefault on a List(List(T)) does not compile] `nested.copyAtDefault(index: 0).copyAtDefault(index: 0).x` emits `&(rae_core_List_copyAtDefault_...(&nested, 0))` — the address of an rvalue — inside the fast-path `__rae_flist` statement expression, so clang fails (`cannot take the address of an rvalue of type rae_List_rae_Point`). The fast path must bind a returned List to a temporary first (and drop it after: the copy owns heap). Add a fixture under the leak check. — DONE: emit_list_fast_access (c_expr.c) now takes the fast path only when the list is a place (ident, member, index); a list a call returns goes through the library call, which binds the copy to a statement temporary and drops it. Fixture 977_chained_list_copy (chained reads off List(List(Point)) and List(List(Int)), off a returned list, copyAtFallback, 50 iterations in a loop) is leak-clean. Seen on the way: `ret { ... }` (a list literal returned directly) is rejected with "cannot lower collection literal without a declared List element type".
- [x] #89945863 {difficulty:4} [compiler — a list literal in `ret` must carry its type: support `ret List(Point) { ... }`] Rae has no type inference, so a returned list literal names its type the way a returned struct does (AGENTS.md: "In a `ret`, where there is no LHS, the literal carries the type: `ret Point { ... }`"). `ret List(Point) { Point { x: 7, y: 8 } }` already PARSES (AstCollectionLiteral.type holds `List(Point)`) but the lowering ignores it and fails with "cannot lower collection literal without a declared List element type" (c_expr.c AST_EXPR_COLLECTION_LITERAL only reads the expected type). Make the literal's own type drive the lowering (createList/add/drop registration in c_discovery too), and check it against the function's return type in sema like any other value. A BARE `ret { ... }` stays an error — do NOT take the type from the return type — with a diagnostic that says to write `ret List(T) { ... }`. Cover a nested `ret List(List(Point)) { { ... } }` and String elements (detached from the string pool), in a fixture under the leak check; fixture 977_chained_list_copy binds with `let` as the workaround and may switch to the new spelling. — DONE: the written type now drives the literal: infer_expr_type_ref returns it, the c_expr lowering prefers it over the expected type, c_discovery registers createList/add/drop for it, and sema resolves it so `ret List(Point) { ... }` into a `ret List(Int)` function is a type error. A bare `ret { ... }` stays an error, now saying to write `List(T) { ... }`. Fixture 978_ret_typed_list_literal (a struct list, nested List(List(Point)), Strings with an interpolation and a moved local, a typed literal as an argument, a generic `ret List(T) { ... }`) is leak-clean; 977 now uses the new spelling; AGENTS.md states the rule for lists. Seen on the way, not fixed: braces inside a string interpolation (`"{f(points: List(P) { ... })}"`) do not parse, and the List mismatch diagnostic prints mangled names (List_Point vs List_int64_t).

Then lib/physics/ecs: the PhysicsWorld resource (fixed step with a capped accumulator, substeps, the event lists from getContactEvents/getSensorEvents/getBodyEvents, static venue shapes), RigidBody / Collider / CharacterBody components, the push / step / pull / event / character systems in the fixed-step schedule (pull uses the body move events, so sleeping bodies cost nothing), spawnRigidBody / despawnRigidBody (destroy the body before the entity's components are cleared), static venue colliders from MeshData. Bodies carry an EntityId field; events carry entity ids. Box3D's callbacks (custom filter, pre-solve, friction/restitution mixing) are not ported because Rae has no function values: decide whether filter categories/groups and per-material mixing tables cover the ECS needs; if a new language or library surface is needed, write it into the design doc and end the task with [question] instead of guessing. Fixtures: spawn/despawn 1000 bodies with recycled entity ids never touching a live body; two worlds stepped 600 times print identical transforms. Needs P4e, P6 and P7. — Done (2026-10-05). docs/physics-design.md rewritten for the port: header, §1-§4 (the port, nothing to vendor), §5 matching lib/physics/ecs, §6/§7/§8/§9. lib/physics/ecs contents: (1) the Physics resource, named so because the port's world is already PhysicsWorld: fixed step with a capped accumulator, body/shape→entity maps in place of user data, entity event queues, venue body; (2) RigidBody, Collider, CharacterBody; (3) the character, push, step, pull and event systems, plus physicsFixedStep/physicsFrame; (4) spawnRigidBody, add*Collider, despawnRigidBody (body destroyed before components are cleared); (5) addVenueMesh from Mesh3d MeshData, plus addVenueBox; (6) entity queries. CharacterMover gained an up axis (yUp = the sample, bit-exact; the ECS uses zUp). Callbacks decision (§5.6): nothing new needed for §0 — filters/groups/filter joints, tangent velocity, Box3D default mixing plus userMaterialId. Breaking renames: Shape to PhysicsShape and MeshData to PhysicsMeshData, because type names are program-wide and these clashed with lib/ui and lib/Mesh3d. Fixture 987: 1000 bodies, 810 recycled ids, 0 violations. Fixture 988: two worlds, 600 steps, every transform identical. Two compiler bugs found, queued below.
- [x] #36872332 {difficulty:4} [compiler: type names are program-wide] Two modules defining the same type name collide as soon as both are loaded, even when neither opens the other: lib/Mesh3d's MeshData vs lib/physics' MeshData, and lib/ui's Shape vs physics' Shape, made physics' own struct literals resolve to the other module's type. The error is misleading: "unknown field 'bounds' in literal of type 'MeshData'" inside lib/physics/geometry/Mesh.rae. P8 worked around it by renaming the physics types (PhysicsMeshData, PhysicsShape). Decide and implement either module-scoped type names (qualified access through import) or at least a clear duplicate-type diagnostic that names both definitions. Add a fixture with two modules defining the same type name. — Done (2026-10-05). Decision: kept type names program-wide (module-scoped types would be a language change) and made the clash a hard error. The second struct/enum declaration of a name now reports "type 'X' is declared twice: here and at <file>:<line>:<col> — type names are program-wide ... rename one of them". Before, the duplicate was silently dropped. Generic templates and specializations are excluded. Implemented in sema_analyze_module (sema.c). Fixture 989_duplicate_type_name: points/Left and points/Right both declare Point. Documented in docs/module-namespacing.md. Checks: full suite green; all 77 examples compile headless with no duplicate (the multi-file ones with --project). lib still has two same-named pairs that no program loads together: Camera2D (Raylib vs ui/BindingComponents) and GpuTiming (GpuTiming vs gpu/GpuTiming). They would now be rejected if ever combined.
- [x] #95614691 {difficulty:4} [compiler: value binding from a view-returning call emits invalid C] `let transform: Transform3D = componentView(this: transforms, entityId: entityId)` (componentView returns `view T`) passes sema but generates C that assigns a `const rae_Transform3D*` to a `rae_Transform3D`, which clang rejects. Either copy (dereference) or reject in sema with a diagnostic pointing to `let x: view T => ...`. Found in P8's physicsPushSystem, which now uses the view alias. Add a fixture. — Done (2026-10-05). The language already had the rule: a value binding may not take a call that returns a reference, and the diagnostic says to bind the view first ('let source: view T => ...'), then copy. The check read the call's resolved declaration, which a generic call does not have yet in sema (the backend specializes it), so componentView and any generic `ret view T` slipped through to invalid C. sema_call_returns_reference (sema.c) now answers for an unresolved generic call from the program's functions of that name: a reference when every candidate returns one. Fixtures: 991_reject_generic_view_call_copy (the reported componentView case is rejected); 992_view_call_copy_two_step (the spelled-out form copies deeply, String field included, for componentView and a user generic; no leaks). Full suite green; all 77 examples compile.
- [x] #59612998 {difficulty:4} [physics port P9a — single-threaded performance] docs/physics-rae-port-design.md §6, §7 P9 and docs/physics-performance-plan.md §3. Port upstream's benchmark scenes (benchmark/main.c: the large pyramid, many pyramids, the joint grid, the convex pile, rain) as Rae programs in benchmarks/physics printing ms per step; compare with the oracle's C scalar and SIMD builds (~/.cache/rae/box3d-oracle/bench-scalar|bench-simd, docs/physics-performance-plan.md §9) on the same machine; target the Rae port within 1.2x of C scalar single-threaded at the release profile, 1.5x being the failure line. Profile and fix in Rae: known costs are whole-struct copies in the solver (warmStartContactsWide copies each ContactConstraintWide, the prepare lanes copy Contact and Manifold, finalize copies BodySim/BodyState/Body), List copyAtDefault/Array copyAtFallback per lane in gather/scatter, and per-step allocations (StepContext and its lists are rebuilt every step; RAE_MEM_STATS should show zero allocations in a steady step). Use the inlining and math-intrinsic tasks' results and the Float4 path; every change keeps fixtures 948-959 bit-exact. Record the numbers and the remaining gap in the design doc. Needs P4-P6 and the Float4 switch.— Done (2026-10-05), but the target is NOT met. benchmarks/physics: rae/Main.rae ports large_pyramid, many_pyramids, joint_grid and convex_pile from shared/benchmarks.c; it prints ms per step and a position checksum. run.sh builds the release profile and runs the oracle's C scalar and SIMD apps, best of three. Ms per step (Rae / C scalar / C SIMD): large 14.84 / 11.05 / 8.10 (1.34x); many 30.84 / 21.23 / 15.09 (1.45x); joint grid 30.12 / 11.68 / 11.61 (2.58x); convex pile 34.44 / 13.79 / 11.60 (2.50x). The large pyramid started at 2267 ms/step. Fixes, all by reading through views instead of copying: - whole SolverSet copies per contact/body (World.setBodySimAt and friends); - Contact deep copies per prepare lane (PrepareLane holds only the read scalars; Manifold.points is now an inline Array(ManifoldPoint, cap: 4)); - ContactConstraintWide copies in warm start and store impulses; the span list; shapes in collide; BodySim in integrate velocities; JointSim in the threshold check. Two compiler fixes found on the way: - c_expr.c: a member access's object no longer inherits the expected type, so `arr.copyAtFallback(...).x` passed as an Int argument specializes Array on its element type (fixture 993); - c_stmt.c: a void method call is judged by its receiver type, so `list.add(value: x)` no longer gets a String-pool mark/flush pair when another `add` returns a value (822 → 422 pool marks in the benchmark). Fixtures 948-988 bit-exact; benchmark checksums unchanged. Full suite green (673 pass); all 77 examples compile headless. Fixture code that read Manifold.points with copyAtDefault (957-959, 969, 974, 980, 982, 983) now uses copyAtFallback. Numbers and causes: docs/physics-rae-port-design.md §6.1 and docs/physics-performance-plan.md §3. Rain is not ported (it needs the ragdoll and the torus mesh). It and the remaining gap are split into the items below. Version 0.1.148.
- [x] #84577109 {difficulty:4} [physics port P9a-2 — joint memory layout] docs/physics-rae-port-design.md §6.1. JointSim is 1816 bytes: Rae has no union, so it carries every joint kind's data (distance, revolute, prismatic, wheel, spherical, motor, parallel, weld) side by side, where Box3D's union is a few hundred bytes. The joint grid runs at 2.58x C scalar (30.1 vs 11.7 ms/step) because the solve streams ~4x the memory per joint. Design a compact layout without a language change, e.g. per-kind data in per-kind lists indexed from a small JointSim, or one shared slot struct sized to the largest kind used, read through views. Keep fixture 984 and the benchmark checksum (sh benchmarks/physics/run.sh) bit-exact. Target: joint_grid within 1.2x of C scalar. Record the numbers in §6.1. — Done (2026-10-05). The 1.2x target is NOT met: joint_grid went from 30.1 to 15.5 ms/step, 2.58x to 1.33x C scalar (11.7). Layout: the eight kinds' records moved out of JointSim into the world's JointKindData (JointData.rae): one list per kind with a free-slot stack. JointSim (1816 → 208 bytes) and Joint carry the record's dataIndex; destroy frees the slot. The record stays put while the sim moves between sets and colours. Each kind's prepare/warmStart/solve takes the record as a mod/view parameter instead of copying it in and back. jointReaction reads world.jointData. Fixture 984 reads the records through world.jointData; it is bit-exact (8192 ops). Benchmark checksums unchanged. Full suite green (673). Also fixed the benchmark timing to match upstream's app: the first step is untimed and both sides divide by steps - 1. §6.1 re-measured all scenes: large 1.32x, many 1.32x, joint 1.33x, convex 2.50x. The remaining joint gap is not layout. The spherical solve compiles to Box3D's instruction count, but the run retires 19% more instructions at a lower IPC (per-joint bounds checks, kind dispatch, BodyState copies). Split into "[physics port P9a-5 — joint solve overhead]". Version 0.1.149.
- [x] #67210012 {difficulty:4} [physics port P9a-5 — joint solve overhead] docs/physics-rae-port-design.md §6.1. P9a-2 moved the joint kinds' records out of JointSim; the joint grid is now 1.33x C scalar (15.5 vs 11.7 ms/step) and the gap is no longer layout. The spherical solve compiles to Box3D's instruction count (~1140 instructions), but the whole run retires 19% more instructions (20.3G vs 17.1G) at a lower IPC (3.7 vs 4.6; /usr/bin/time -l). Suspects: per joint, jointBodyState copies each 64-byte BodyState (copyAtDefault: zero-init then copy) and storeJointVelocities re-indexes the list, where Box3D points at the state; the kind dispatch inlines all eight kinds into one 8500-instruction solveJoint; bounds-checked modAt on the colour's sims and on the kind list; a second pass over each block for the joint-event thresholds; prepare copies each JointSim out and sets it back. Measure each (build /tmp copies with noinline, compare against the C app on the same machine) and fix in Rae, bit-exact (fixture 984, benchmark checksums). Target: joint_grid within 1.2x of C scalar (sh benchmarks/physics/run.sh). — Done (2026-10-05). Target met: joint_grid 13.44 vs 11.56 ms/step = 1.16x C scalar (run.sh at load ~3.4; it was 15.5 ms / 1.33x). Measured by patching the generated C in /tmp and counting instructions and cycles (setup subtracted, configs interleaved with the C app, minimum taken). Three fixes landed in lib/physics/dynamics: (1) SphericalJoint's solve writes the point-to-point mass out inline, as Box3D does; clang would not inline the pointToPointMass call into the eight-kind dispatch. (2) solveJointsBlock skips the joint-event threshold pass when no joint of the block has a threshold. (3) Prepare in place: prepareColorJoint reads the bodies first, then prepares the sim and its record where they live. The JointSim copy-out/set-back is gone, and so is colorJointSim. Tried without gain: an unchecked BodyState read in jointBodyState (fewer instructions, no fewer cycles) and out-of-line per-kind functions. Fixture 984 and all 29 physics fixtures (948-988) bit-exact; benchmark checksums unchanged. The full suite was not rerun: the change is confined to lib/physics, which those fixtures cover. §6.1 table re-measured: large 1.18x, many 1.35x, joint 1.16x, convex 2.50x.
- [x] #70521881 {difficulty:4} [physics port P9a-3 — allocation-free step and narrow phase] docs/physics-rae-port-design.md §6.1. RAE_MEM_STATS counts ~3400 buffer allocations (~28 MB) per steady large-pyramid step. Sources: (1) StepContext and its lists, wideConstraints first, are rebuilt from empty every step in WorldStep.stepWorld; keep them on the world and clear them instead, which needs a way to lend the world-owned context to solve(world: mod, context: mod) without aliasing — design it, and ask if it needs a language feature; (2) the hull narrow phase allocates Lists per pair (SeparatingAxis.faceDots, edgeCandidates, Manifold.clipPolygon, LocalManifold points) where Box3D uses stack arrays; use Array(T, cap: N) or per-task scratch. The convex pile runs at 2.50x C scalar (34.4 vs 13.8 ms/step), mostly in the narrow phase. Goal: zero buffer allocations in a steady step, convex_pile within 1.2x. Keep fixtures 948-988 and the benchmark checksums bit-exact. — Done (2026-10-05). Allocation goal met; the convex pile target is NOT (1.54x, from 2.50x). A steady large-pyramid step went from 431 buffer allocations to 0. Measured with an allocation-counting runtime: full run minus a 2-step run. (1) StepContext: needs no language feature. The world owns stepContext and stepWorld lends it with own moves (`var context: StepContext = own world.stepContext`, resetStepContext, then moved back). PairContext (new module), the tree's rebuild/copy/leaf stacks, collide's contact list and solve's colour plans work the same way. The rebuild's node-array swap and the clip-polygon swap now move instead of deep-copying. (2) Narrow phase: equal hulls are shared between shapes, as Box3D's hull database does (addSharedHull, HullContent; the biggest win, 31 → 23 ms). SAT/clip/point lists live in a per-task HullScratch. Whole-struct copies of TaskContext, GraphColor, PhysicsShape and Contact are replaced by views or field moves. Convex pile 34.4 → 21.2 ms/step vs C 13.7. The remaining gap is Box3D's SoA hull copies and four-wide SAT loops; split into "[physics port P9a-6 — SoA hulls and four-wide SAT]". Contact/island creation still allocates while a pile settles: "[physics port P9a-7 — allocation-free contact and island churn]". Table (load ~9): large 1.15x, many 1.26x, joint 1.14x, convex 1.54x. Fixtures 948-988 and benchmark checksums bit-exact; full suite green (673, leak checks included). Docs §6.1 updated. Version 0.1.150.
- [x] #98967804 {difficulty:4} [physics port P9a-6 — SoA hulls and four-wide SAT] docs/physics-rae-port-design.md §6.1. After P9a-3 (shared hulls, HullScratch, an allocation-free step) the convex pile is 1.54x C scalar (21.2 vs 13.7 ms/step) and the rest is arithmetic in the hull SAT. Box3D's b3HullData carries structure-of-arrays copies of the points and the face normals (b3GetHullSoaVertices/Normals). Its support search (b3FindHullSupportVertex), face separation (b3GetFaceSeparation) and edge-pair loop (b3NegativeTransformFromSoA plus the four-lane edge test) run four lanes at a time on b3FloatW, which clang vectorises even in the BOX3D_DISABLE_SIMD build. The port's Hull has only AoS points and scalar loops: findHullSupportVertex 11% and testEdgePairs 10% of the convex pile. Add SoA point/normal lists to Hull (built by HullCreate and cloneAndTransformHull, included in HullContent's comparison), and port those loops onto lib/Float4 with Box3D's lane order and tie-breaking. Keep fixtures 951/952 and the benchmark checksums bit-exact. Target: convex_pile within 1.2x of C scalar (sh benchmarks/physics/run.sh). — Done (2026-10-05). The 1.2x target is NOT met: convex_pile 19.69 vs 13.77 ms/step = 1.43x (from 1.54x). Hull has SoA points/normals (buildHullSoa, called by createHull, cloneAndTransformHull and makeTransformedBoxHull; compared by HullContent). findHullSupportVertex, supportWide (b3GetSupportWide with Float4.embedIndex/minLaneIndex) and faceDots run on lib/Float4 in Box3D's order and tie-breaking; the generated loop is plain NEON. Also: one EdgeOfA record per candidate edge in testEdgePairs, and collide reads a 72-byte ContactBodyPose instead of two BodySim copies. The support search now costs what C's does. The rest of the gap is spread evenly (every narrow-phase part ~1.4x C; 25% more instructions at lower IPC), split into "[physics port P9a-8 — narrow-phase per-access overhead]". Fixtures 951/952 and all 29 physics fixtures bit-exact; benchmark checksums unchanged; full suite green (673). Table (load ~9): large 1.15x, many 1.27x, joint 1.15x, convex 1.43x. Docs §6.1.
- [x] #49186807 {difficulty:4} [physics port P9a-8 — narrow-phase per-access overhead] docs/physics-rae-port-design.md §6.1. After P9a-6 (SoA hulls, Float4 support search) the convex pile is 1.43x C scalar (19.7 vs 13.8 ms/step), and every part of the hull narrow phase is ~1.4x C's: 25% more instructions at a lower IPC (/usr/bin/time -l: 77.8G vs 62.0G instructions, IPC 2.67 vs 2.89). Suspects: - bounds-checked List element reads (copyAtDefault/viewAt) in edgeCandidates, the face loops of computeSeparatingAxis, clipPolygon, findIncidentFace and collideTask, where C indexes raw arrays; - HullHalfEdge/HullVertex/HullFace fields stored as 8-byte Int where Box3D uses uint8 (a half-edge is 32 bytes against 4); - HullHalfEdge and Plane struct copies (hullEdge/hullPlane); - collideTask's own body (12% of the step against C's 6% for b3CollideTask). Measure each with /tmp builds that patch the generated C (count instructions and cycles, interleaved with the C app), then fix in Rae or in the compiler's List access codegen, bit-exact (fixtures 948-988, benchmark checksums). Target: convex_pile within 1.2x of C scalar (sh benchmarks/physics/run.sh). — Done (2026-10-05). Target met, on the line: convex_pile 16.48 vs 13.71 ms/step = 1.20x C scalar (run.sh, load ~3; from 1.43x). Measured by patching the generated C. Removing every List bounds check cut instructions 152M → 128M per step but cycles only 1%. 8-bit hull indices, a 32-bit Contact or TreeNode, -O3 and -mcpu changed nothing. The gap was memory latency. Box3D prefetches its contact 4 iterations ahead in b3CollideTask; the port did not. New List.prefetch(index:) in lib/core: a no-result cache hint, rae_ext_rae_buf_prefetch in rae_runtime.h, fixture 994, documented in docs/collections.md. collideTask now prefetches 4 contacts ahead (19.4 → 16.8 ms); the wide prepare prefetches the next wide constraint's contacts (~2%); the candidate flush mirrors Box3D's slot and shape prefetches. Compiler: a sizeof(T) argument no longer wraps calls in a String-pool mark/flush pair. Pyramids gained too. Table: large 0.98x, many 1.06x, joint 1.13x, convex 1.20x. Physics fixtures and benchmark checksums bit-exact. Full suite: 673 pass; the TSan 106_mobile_ui timeout under load ~18 passed on rerun (tsan-check: 10/10). Version 0.1.151; docs §6.1.

Large pyramid steady step: still 0. Fixes: - destroyContact reads its contact through a view (it copied it, ~6 900/step); - continuous collision uses world-owned hits/fractions/query-stack lists (beginTreeQueryWith; ~29 000/step); - collideHulls' edge manifold lives in HullScratch; - the created pair keys go to world.createdPairKeys (updateBroadPhasePairs no longer returns a list; fixture 956 reads the field); - a new contact reuses its slot's lists (zeroContactWithLists); - mergeIslands/destroyIsland/trySleepIsland move island lists instead of copying islands; the sleep id list is a world list; - begin/end-touch handling reads contacts through views. Compiler fix: `own` of a mod parameter emitted the pointer (fixture 995). Remaining 4/step (ShapeProxy point list per TOI, QuickSort stack) split into "[physics port P9a-9 — fixed-size proxies and sort stack]". Fixtures 948-988 and benchmark checksums bit-exact; full suite 675 pass. Version 0.1.152.
- [x] #57674387 {difficulty:4} [physics port P9a-9 — fixed-size proxies and sort stack] docs/physics-rae-port-design.md §6.1. After P9a-7 a moving pile allocates ~4 buffers per step: makeShapeProxy builds a List(Vec3) per time-of-impact query (Continuous.shapeTimeOfImpact; Box3D's b3ShapeProxy is a fixed array of B3_MAX_SHAPE_CAST_POINTS), and QuickSort.quickSortWithCompanion creates its range stack per call (from updateBroadPhasePairs). Give ShapeProxy a fixed Array (or a reused scratch list) and the sort a caller-owned stack, then measure with the allocation-counting runtime: count buffer allocations per caller, full run minus a shorter run, and check that the shorter run's C build succeeds. Goal: zero allocations once a pile has stopped creating contacts. Bit-exact: fixtures 948-988, benchmark checksums. — Done (2026-10-05). Convex pile steps 460-500: 4.1 → 0.05 buffer allocations per step (2 in 40 steps). Both are the capacity of collide's world-owned contact list growing with the contact count, so a pile that stops creating contacts allocates nothing. Measured with the allocation-counting runtime, full run minus a shorter run; the shorter run's C build is checked (the script exits on a failed build). Time-of-impact proxies use two world-owned point lists lent through solveContinuous → queryContinuous → continuousCandidate → shapeTimeOfImpact (ProxyPoints; shapeProxyPoints fills them; moved into ToiInput and back). This uses the reused-list option rather than a fixed Array, because ShapeProxy.points is a List across GJK and shape casts. The pair-key sort uses world.pairSortStack via the new QuickSort.quickSortWithStack (quickSortWithCompanion keeps its signature). Fixtures 948-988 bit-exact (29/29 physics fixtures); no leaks in 953/958/983 under RAE_MEM_STATS; benchmark checksums unchanged. Docs §6.1.
- [x] #35854297 {difficulty:4} [physics port P9a-4 — contact layout in collide and prepare] docs/physics-rae-port-design.md §6.1. On the pyramids (1.34x / 1.45x C scalar) Rae's SIMD solve and push already beat C scalar, but collide and the wide prepare cost ~2.5x C's. Box3D iterates each colour's contiguous b3ContactSim array; the port reaches each contact by id in world.contacts (a 432-byte Contact with its manifold in a separate heap List) and copies two 224-byte BodySims per contact in collide (resolveContactBodySim). Measure and fix: read the bodies through views (or a slim pose struct), and consider storing the solver-facing contact data (manifold, friction, restitution, body indices) contiguously per colour as Box3D does. Target: large_pyramid and many_pyramids within 1.2x of C scalar, bit-exact (fixtures 948-988, benchmark checksums). — Done (2026-10-05). The body copies were replaced earlier: collide reads a 72-byte ContactBodyPose (P9a-6), and collide and the wide prepare prefetch their contacts (P9a-8). Per stage, the pyramids' collide is now 1.38x/1.49x C's (not 2.5x), and solve+finalize 1.01x/1.02x. Collide is ~14% of the step, so the contiguous per-colour contact layout would recover at most ~0.4 ms/step; not done, with the reason recorded in §6.1. No code changes; docs only.

Per stage the extra ~1 ms/step is in solve; continuous collision never runs in this scene. Find the cause (e.g. patch the 6c0b69e6 C file back function by function, or compare `sample` per-instruction), fix it bit-exact, and re-record the table. — Done (2026-10-05). Cause: the step did not change. P9a-7's mergeIslands stopped copying both islands to compare sizes; those setup copies had steered which pages libmalloc's large-allocation cache handed out. A single joint-solve pass reads about 10 MB (JointSim + SphericalJointData per joint, plus body states) against the M1 Max's 12 MB physically indexed L2, so page placement decided whether it stayed in cache. Shown by transplanting 2d3af2ed's mergeIslands into 6c0b69e6's C (13.7 ms), and by restoring only the copying size comparison at HEAD (13.2 ms). Proven independent of the step code: a 256 MB allocate-and-free at start-up made 6c0b69e6 fast (13.0 ms); 16 MB, or MallocLargeCache=0, made 2d3af2ed slow (17.0/16.2). Also ruled out: code padding before the solver (0-2048 bytes); moving the hot arrays to fresh malloc or mmap; 12 random large-allocation paddings; identical joint/body order and identical array bytes. Fix: SphericalJointData.indexA/indexB are Int32, as Box3D's ints are (272 -> 260 bytes, b3SphericalJoint's size). joint_grid is now 12.3-13.4 ms whatever the start-up history; run.sh 12.97 ms = 1.09x C scalar, all checksums unchanged. Physics fixtures 29/29 plus 996 bit-exact; full suite 676/0. Docs §6.1 table re-recorded and the open note replaced.

Move the warning into one out-of-line helper (List.set has the same shape) so the in-range path is a compare and a store. Full suite 676/0.
- [x] #93085550 {difficulty:4} [physics track A, P9b — the parallel step of the Rae port] docs/physics-two-implementations.md §5 step 1 (two tracks: this is the port, track A). docs/parallel-stages-design.md (Option C, decided and implemented: a stage is one parallelLoop, `unsafe` for the colouring's disjoint writes, RAE_PARALLEL_CHECK verifies them) and docs/physics-performance-plan.md §5, §7. Thread the step without changing the solver tasks: (1) StageDispatcher.executeMainStage runs a stage's blocks as one parallelLoop (claimBlock/addCompletions/resetCompletions become no-ops or go away; one-block stages stay inline); (2) the parallel-for sites of the step — pair finding (BroadPhasePairs' cross and self tasks), collide (collideTask), finalize (finalizeBodies), sensors (sensorTask), bullets — become parallelLoops whose per-chunk results (pair keys, contact state bits, awake island bits, split candidates, sensor event bits, bullet lists, hit bits) live in chunk scratch merged in chunk order, replacing the one TaskContext with per-chunk contexts; (3) world.workerCount from the pool. Determinism fixture: every body transform and every event list identical at 1, 2, 4 and 8 workers on the pyramid and the events scenes, and still bit-exact against the oracle at workerCount 1; run under RAE_PARALLEL_CHECK and the TSan gate (add the fixture to its CASES). Record the speedup curve next to Box3D C's at the same worker counts (docs/physics-performance-plan.md §2). Needs P9a. — Done (2026-10-05). (1) StageDispatcher.executeMainStage runs a stage's blocks as one parallelLoop (unsafe; the join is the stage barrier); claimBlock/addCompletions/resetCompletions/executeStage/workerStartIndex are gone; single-block stages and workerCount 1 run inline. Block reports go to TaskContext slot = block index: prepare's anyRestitution (now returned, OR-ed after the prepare stages), joint threshold bits, scalar and wide hit bits. (2) Pair finding (each chunk has its own PairContext in its TaskContext; keys concatenated, then sorted as before), collide, finalize, sensors and bullets run as parallelLoops over chunks of their blocks (ParallelFor.chunkFirstBlock). TaskContext is now per chunk slot, 4 per worker. Shared writes moved to chunk scratch and merged in chunk order: bullet lists; broad-phase marks (deferred, applied serially after finalize; non-bullets only sweep the static tree, so the tree is identical); continuous collision's working lists (moved off the world); sensor hits. sleepIslands now ORs every chunk's awake-island bits. (3) world.workerCount = def.workerCount clamped to [1, 64] (Box3D's rule); the ECS default is Parallel.workerCount(); benchmarks/physics takes a workers argument. Determinism: new fixture 997 (300-box pyramid plus events scene with contacts/hits/sensors/joint thresholds/bullets/sleep) is identical at 1, 2, 4 and 8 workers. It is on the TSan gate's CASES and clean; reverting one slot fix makes TSan report 3 races. The oracle replays 958/959/983/984 now run at 1 and at 8 workers, bit-exact both times. Physics fixtures 29/29 + 996; full suite green except 997's missing config.cmd, since added and passing through the runner; TSan gate 11/11. Curve recorded next to Box3D C in docs/physics-performance-plan.md §2b (load ~6). Rae vs C scalar at 1/2/4/8 workers: large pyramid 12.9/6.7/3.9/4.1 vs 11.7/6.6/3.8/3.4; joint grid 13.5/7.0/3.9/3.4 vs 11.8/6.3/3.4/2.6; convex 16.7/10.2/6.6/5.3 vs 14.1/7.6/4.3/3.1; rain 6.2/4.6/4.1/5.6 vs 5.0/3.0/1.8/1.5. Single-worker time unchanged (interleaved old/new within 1%). Version 0.1.154. Split out (queued before the performance pass): "[runtime — per-thread allocation counters …]", "[physics track A — the mesh narrow phase allocation-free]", "[physics track A — the step's serial fraction at 8 workers]".

Each case is listed in docs/physics-two-implementations.md §4. lib/webgpu regenerates byte-identical except 3 fixes in WebgpuTypes (int fields mapped; the nested-union struct is no longer emitted as a bogus `type data`). - [x] #19451472 {difficulty:4} [compiler — `ret componentView(...)` from a function that returns a value type emits the reference: gcc fails] In a function declared `ret Transform3D`, `ret componentView(this: table, entityId: id)` (componentView returns `view T`) passes sema, and the C backend emits `rae_Transform3D __ret_val = <const rae_Transform3D*>` ("initializing 'rae_Transform3D' with an expression of incompatible type 'const rae_Transform3D *'"). Found in lib/physicsScenes/port/VehicleSpawn.rae chassisTransform, worked around with componentCopy. Either copy out of the view on return (view T returned as T copies, like a `let x: T = viewExpr`) or reject it in sema with a clear message; add a fixture. — Done (2026-10-05). Chose the reject option, since the language already rejects the `let` form: spec 2.3.1 refuses `let t: T = <reference-returning call>` with "copying what it refers to must be spelled out", and a value `ret` is the same implicit copy. Copying on `ret` would have made `ret` quietly do what `let` refuses. Sema's ret case (compiler/src/sema.c) now raises "this call returns a reference, and this function returns a value; ... bind it first ('let source: view T => ...'), then 'ret source'" for a plain, a generic or a componentView call and for an `opt T` return; forwarding with `ret view` stays legal. Both forms failed in gcc before. Spec 2.3.1 gained the ret paragraph. Fixtures: 1003_reject_ret_reference_call_as_value (the four rejected forms, forwarding legal) and 1004_ret_copy_from_named_view (the spelled-out repair runs; a String-carrying struct is deep-copied and independent; nothing outstanding at exit). All 80 active examples still pass sema+emit. Full suite 655/0. Version 0.1.163.

- [x] #81739763 {difficulty:4} [compiler — `own` out of an opt field does not empty the source: use after free, double free] Found while merging the shadow and G-buffer submissions (docs/physics-performance-plan.md §10d). Moving a List FIELD with `own` zeroes the source (the C does copy + memset), but moving an `opt` field does not: `holder.second = own holder.first` (both `opt List(Int)`) emits `__asg = holder.first; drop(&holder.second); holder.second = __asg;`, so `holder.first` still owns the buffer, and a following `holder.first = none` (or holder's own drop) frees what `holder.second` now holds. Repro: type Holder { first: opt List(Int) second: opt List(Int) }; var holder: Holder = { first: items, second: none }; holder.second = own holder.first; holder.first = none; then read holder.second (reads freed memory; in the renderer it aborted with 'pointer being freed was not allocated'). Make `own` of an opt (or any owning) field empty its source like the List case, and add a fixture that checks it under RAE_MEM_STATS (and ASan if the runner has it). — Done: c_expr.c's AST_EXPR_OWN emptying move excluded opt places (`!ptr->is_opt`); a zeroed opt is none, so the same copy + memset now covers them. Fixture 1008_own_opt_field_move (opt List move then clearing the source, the swap idiom through a local, an opt String moved and its owner dropped) failed before (source still held the list, then garbage and a crash) and passes after under the runner's RAE_MEM_STATS leak check; also clean under -fsanitize=address,undefined by hand (the runner has no ASan mode). Suite 659/0; version 0.1.177.

- [x] #70977507 {difficulty:4} [web runtime — a browser frame callback instead of Asyncify for the frame loop] Found while bringing the physics playground to the browser (docs/physics-performance-plan.md §10g): the WASM build keeps Rae's blocking `loop not pollClose()` shape with -sASYNCIFY, which instruments the call paths and makes the port's physics step ~1.2x slower in Chrome (~20 ms/step at 5000 boxes) than the same WASM in Node (16.4). docs/webgpu-3d-renderer.md already names the plan: a first-class frame-callback API (requestAnimationFrame calling one Rae frame function) that replaces the Asyncify layer without changing renderer APIs. Design it (ownership of the app state between callbacks, how examples' loops opt in), port one example (122_physics_playground_port), and compare the step and frame with RAE_PLAYGROUND_PROFILE via index.html?RAE_… query params. — Measured first (docs/web-frame-loop.md): the browser slowdown was the missing -msimd128, not Asyncify. In Chrome at 5000 boxes, physics 34.3 -> 19.3-20.2 ms per frame (~22 -> ~12.7 ms per step) and frame 45-52 -> 35 ms with SIMD; dropping Asyncify's indirect-call instrumentation saved only ~4% of the step (~15% of the frame, ~17% of size) and is unsafe if a pointer-called function ever yields, so it stays off. -msimd128 is now on for every browser build (smoke test passes). The frame-callback API is designed (state owned by one value between frames; options A: a Gpu2d.runFrames(state:, frame: <function name>) intrinsic, B: a setup/frame entry convention, C: keep Asyncify) but NOT implemented: Rae has no function values, so each option is new language semantics needing a pick; queued as a decide-first task.
- [x] #42991612 {difficulty:4} [web runtime — implement the browser frame callback (decide first: docs/web-frame-loop.md §4)] The design is written and measured (docs/web-frame-loop.md): Asyncify now costs only ~4% of the playground's step and ~15% of its frame since -msimd128 fixed the real slowdown. Implementing a frame callback needs new semantics because Rae has no function values: pick option A (a Gpu2d.runFrames(state: own T, frame: <function name>) intrinsic, recommended), B (a setup/frame entry convention) or C (keep Asyncify). Then implement it (native: a plain loop; web: emscripten_set_main_loop_arg over a heap box of the state and a generated trampoline, no -sASYNCIFY for apps that use it), port 122_physics_playground_port's runWindowed to setup + playgroundFrame (§5), and compare with RAE_PLAYGROUND_PROFILE via index.html?RAE_… in headless Chrome. — QUESTION: Which form should the browser frame callback take (docs/web-frame-loop.md §4)? A: `ret Gpu2d.runFrames(state: own app, frame: playgroundFrame)`, an intrinsic whose `frame:` argument is a function name checked to be `func(state: mod T) ret Bool` (recommended: explicit at the call site, one loop shape for native and web; the one place a function name is an argument). B: an entry convention, where `setup() ret T` plus `frame(state: mod T) ret Bool` in the entry module replace main's loop. C: keep Asyncify (with -msimd128 it costs ~4% of the step, ~15% of the frame). Each of A/B is new language semantics (Rae has no function values), so nothing was implemented; with a pick, the implementation and the 122 port in §5 are ready to start. ANSWER (in chat): option B, a fixed-name entry `func setup() ret T` + `func frame(app: mod T) ret Bool` instead of main; no function values. DONE: generated main (native loop; web emscripten_set_main_loop_arg over a heap box, -DRAE_WEB_FRAME_CALLBACK, -sASYNCIFY_IGNORE_INDIRECT=1, no per-frame yield or blocking present poll); fixture 1010; 122/123 ported to PlaygroundState setup/frame. Chrome, 5000 boxes: ~25 ms -> ~18.6 ms wall per frame (~40 -> ~54 fps), .wasm 2.52 -> 2.13 MB. Limit: no blocking GPU readback inside a web frame (docs/web-frame-loop.md §5-§6).
- [x] #65188044 {difficulty:4} [language — the `mainLoop` keyword replaces the setup/frame entry convention (decision 2026-10-06)] The goal is one entry point (`main`) and no special function names, so the fixed-name `func setup() ret T` + `func frame(app: mod T) ret Bool` entry (docs/web-frame-loop.md §5, `emit_frame_entry` in compiler/src/c_backend.c) is replaced by a loop keyword in the `loop`/`parallelLoop` family: `var state: PlaygroundState = createPlayground()` then `mainLoop state { if Gpu2d.pollClose() { break } stepAndDrawPlayground(state: state) }` then `ret 0`. Semantics: `state` is one owned value moved into the loop and dropped when it ends; the body runs once per frame and `break` ends it; the body may use only `state` and constants (no other locals of main; compiler-checked, like parallelLoop bodies); `mainLoop` is allowed only in `main` and must be its last statement, followed only by `ret <Int>` (that value is the native exit code; ignored in the browser, where main returns before the first frame). Lowering: the compiler outlines the body into a C function over the state (reuse the parallelLoop outlining); natively a plain `while` loop then the drop; on the web a heap box + emscripten_set_main_loop_arg over a generated trampoline, keeping the existing RAE_FRAME_CALLBACK_ENTRY marker -> -DRAE_WEB_FRAME_CALLBACK + -sASYNCIFY_IGNORE_INDIRECT=1 link path. Remove the setup/frame convention entirely (codegen, the `entry_file_path` lookup if unused, AGENTS.md note). Port 122/123 (identical Main.rae) back to a `main` that keeps its headless modes as plain code (no Sys.exit in setup) and ends with `mainLoop`; rewrite fixture 1010 to `mainLoop` and add diagnostics fixtures (mainLoop outside main, statements after it, body using another local, wrong state type). Update docs/web-frame-loop.md §4-§6 with the decision and the new shape; re-measure in headless Chrome at 5000 boxes (expect the same ~18.6 ms/frame as the setup/frame build). Bump compiler/VERSION PATCH. DONE: `mainLoop state { }` keyword (lexer/parser/printer), rules in compiler/src/sema_main_loop.c (only in main, last statement, `var` struct state, body sees only the state, no ret), lowering emit_main_loop in c_stmt.c (outlined body; native while + drop; web heap box + emscripten_set_main_loop_arg, same fast link path); setup/frame convention and entry_file_path removed; 122/123 back on main + mainLoop; fixtures 1010_main_loop and 1011_main_loop_rejects; docs/web-frame-loop.md §4-§6 + AGENTS.md; 0.1.184. Chrome 5000 boxes on a loaded machine: mainLoop 25.8-28.0 ms frame work vs setup/frame 26.2 vs Asyncify loop 34.9 (the afternoon 18.6 ms figure was an idle machine).

With all four examples (122/123 playground, 124/125 vehicle) and both performance passes done, measure and write §6 of the doc: speed (benchmarks/physics gains a track-B column; step ms per scene at 1, 2, 4, 8 workers; playground frame time at stress scale; the WASM builds in the browser), behaviour (determinism hashes across worker counts; equality of the tracks at the same pin, scalar and SIMD), cost to own (Rae and C lines each track adds, build time, binary size, and the cost of moving to upstream HEAD: do it for track B on a branch and report what broke; for track A measure the size of the upstream diff the port would have to port), and what each makes possible (callbacks, own solver changes, single-language debugging, the compiler work the port drives). Do not remove either track in this task. MEASURED (docs/physics-two-implementations.md §6): track B (Box3D C, SIMD) is 1.1-1.5x faster per step at 1-8 workers, ~1.35x at 10000 boxes in the playground, ~1.6x in the browser (8.7 vs 13-15 ms/step; B now builds for the browser too); every playground scene hashes identically across both tracks, Box3D scalar and SIMD, and 1/7 workers; A owns 45k Rae lines vs B 4.7k Rae + 0.6k C, clean build 94 s vs 39 s; moving B to upstream HEAD took ~30 min on branch trial/box3d-upstream-head (13-line binding diff, one bit-exact golden broke), A would have to port ~2-3k lines of contact/manifold changes per such batch. Recommendation: B as Rae's engine; A frozen at pin 9f998c8 as a compiler benchmark/test. QUESTION: Which track does Rae use, and what happens to the other: retire it, keep it as a frozen benchmark (recommended for A), or keep it as a second backend? ANSWER (2026-10-07): track B is the engine; track A is kept as a frozen benchmark and reference (keeps compiling, nothing new ported until decided). Applied: docs/physics-two-implementations.md §7, the FROZEN notice in lib/physics/README.md (ported from 9f998c8, 2026-09-27; the commits not ported listed), and the port's own pin tools/box3d-oracle/pin, separate from track B's tools/box3d/pin.
